The outlet notch and the distributary grooves do not take it, and not because the ground there is bare: a notch is a spill's base-level fall spent over a fixed reach to grade a sill down to the water, rather than a cell deciding how much of itself the water passing over it takes away, and an erodibility multiplier has nothing to scale in the first. With `VegetationConfig.enabled` off the climate stage hands back a zero density, and a density of zero is a shielding factor of one. **The march runs twice, on measurement.** The design asked for one march at the top unless the rainfall drifted more than a tenth of the land mean across the rounds. Measured on the weight the rounds actually route with, on terrain evolved with the feed *on* as well as on uniform-rain terrain: top to end it drifts **42.5%, 34.3% and 43.7%** on seeds 7/42/1234 at 512 (41.7/33.8/42.8% with the rounds run flat) and **27.6%** on seed 42 at 2048, because twelve rounds take enough off a range to change how much lift it forces out of the wind crossing it. So a second march runs at the midpoint, and it earns its place: from the midpoint's terrain the drift to the end is **17.9%, 15.6% and 18.9%** at 512 and **10.5%** at 2048, well under half. Twice and not per round: the rain shadow's *place* is set by the coastlines and the belts, which no round moves. **Cost, and rule 8 declined.** The whole provisional path - the percentile cut, the still ocean, both seasonal marches, the biomes and the cover - is 281 ms at 512 and 709 ms at 1024, so the pair the stage runs is **7.1% and 6.0% of a whole generation** (7.9 s and 23.6 s on this machine). Dearer than H2's still-ocean climate (66/298/1,408 ms at 512/1024/2048) because that one wanted only a snow balance. Rule 8 is declined for it on that figure rather than on taste: the moisture march is a lock-step wavefront across the wind, a sequential dependency a card cannot widen. **The still ocean, stated with its figure.** Seed 42 at 512, provisional annual rainfall with the gyres solved against without: **56.3 mm RMS over land, 10.0% of the 565 mm land mean**. A simplification, recorded, not revisited here. **What the guards measure.** `ClimateFedErosionTest`, against a control run with `ErosionConfig.climateFeed` off - which is not a weakened stage but the stage as it was. The belt and its flanks had to be written, because nothing in the suite classified one: the largest connected run of cells within `boundaryFalloffCells` of an Andean or collisional boundary standing above 1 km, split by whether the ground rises or falls from the cell the wind reaches first. *The forcing* is asserted on the **first round**, which is the only round the two runs still share a terrain, a set of receivers and a set of gradients, so the only thing that differs is the weather: the windward flank takes **2.33-4.77 times** the leeward's rain, and turning the feed on multiplies its share of that round's incision by **1.57, 1.65, 1.26 and 2.00** against the **1.49, 1.47, 1.22 and 1.75** that `sqrt(P_windward / P_leeward)` at m = 0.5 asks for with a fifth off. The control's own windward-over-leeward ratio is **0.91-1.15** and is deliberately not asserted to be near 1: a belt's two flanks are not mirror images - the windward one is the one the ocean is on, so it is shorter, steeper and younger - and flat rain over an asymmetric range cuts its two sides unequally for reasons that have nothing to do with rain. *The cover* is asserted **cell by cell**, not as a band ratio: on the ~93,000 cells a seed leaves unclamped by the drop/2 and above-sea caps, the quotient of the shielded incision over the bare one equals the relative factor to within **7.7e-08**. A band ratio could not have tested it - `1 - 0.5 * density` over cells at density >= 0.6 against <= 0.1 lands anywhere in about 0.50-0.74 depending on how the density sits inside each band, so a bar at Istanbulluoglu and Bras's half would fail a correct implementation as readily as a wrong one; the band ratio is **0.58-0.61** here, printed beside the cover means (0.75-0.79 against 0.01-0.05) that explain it. *Dissection follows rainfall*: Spearman rank correlation of a cell's rainfall against how far it fell, over land on the four standard seeds at 512, **+0.244 to +0.312** against the control's **+0.087 to +0.137**. **What twelve rounds leave is printed and not asserted**, because by then the two terrains have diverged and a difference between them is the whole coupled history rather than the law: relief lost windward over leeward **1.11-1.64** against the control's 0.88-1.28, routed drainage density at a flat support **0.39-0.51** of the leeward flank's (0.41-0.47 with the feed off, so that ordering is the belt's shape and not the rain), and the flanks' own weight ratio falling from 3.24/3.17/2.36/3.99 to 2.25/2.44/1.49/3.28 as the range wears down. Earth's figure sits beside them: the Southern Alps, ten times the rain and 5-10 mm/yr of exhumation against under one (Willett 1999; Hovius, Stark and Allen 1997), an order of magnitude where these are tens of per cent. **The precipitation-weighted talus is out of scope, not impossible.** Water does change where rock fails - pore pressure lowers the effective stress on a slip surface, roots add cohesion above it - so this is a scope decision and not a physical argument. What was refused is the form: a rainfall-driven hillslope flux would be Culling's diffusion, a linear law where the sweeps are a threshold one, and S1 already rejected that as different physics. The defensible term for a threshold law is on the critical slope itself, and it belongs with H3, where the rock gets a lithology to have a critical slope of. **`DRAWN_RIVER` is no longer the river stage's figure in disguise.** Still the same float, 0.0006, and no longer the same quantity: the river stage's is a share of the world's runoff and this one is a share of the land the accumulation weights, which is now rainfall. Under flat rain they coincided. They do not now, and that is the point - the erosion's channel threshold sits where the water is. The comment that said they agreed is corrected. **Reuse and the format.** Erosion now reads `config.climate`, `config.ocean` (through `withoutCurrents`) and `config.vegetation`, so all three are in the erosion stage's guard in `WorldGenerationEngine`, each conditioned on `climateFeed` so that moving a rainfall slider cannot re-cut twelve rounds of valleys a flat-rain world never heard; `IncrementalReuseTest` gains a `climateFeed` variant. `climateFeed` is serialised, so `WorldCodec.FORMAT_VERSION` is 13 with its own history entry and the gzip fixture was regenerated in the same commit - rule 11 of CONVENTIONS.md, and worse than the usual case of it because the key's default is the *new* behaviour, so a format-12 file reopened would be re-cut by a rule it had never heard and nothing would look wrong. **Bit-equality.** Seed 42 at 128, `WorldFingerprintTest`: **2203110568049215329** on 66d81e3 and **2203110568049215329** on this branch with `climateFeed` off, and 6884965881236312070 with it on. Flat rain normalises to all ones, so the accumulation is handed the same 1f it always was; the old world is reproduced exactly, and the flag is shown to move the world. **The cover is spent relatively, and that was found by measuring.** Built first as an absolute multiplier - `incision * (1 - 0.5 * density)` - it took **a third of the world's erosion away**: denudation off an active belt, on `IsostasyTest`'s own instrument pooled over five seeds, fell from **0.271 to 0.189 mm/yr**, and seven cases in seven classes that are green on 66d81e3 went red behind it. The cause is that `bedrockErodibilityPerYear` carries Stock and Montgomery's and Lague's figures for real bedrock rivers, and those rivers ran through forests: the cover is already in the number, and multiplying by it again counts it twice. Istanbulluoglu and Bras compare a vegetated catchment with a bare one and say nothing about either in absolute terms, so a relative half is also the only thing their result licenses. The cure is the one the runoff weight already uses: `(1 - 0.5 * density)` is divided by its own mean over the land, re-taken every pass beside the runoff's, so the land's mean erodibility is exactly 1 and the term moves where the rock comes off rather than how much. Measured after: **0.218 mm/yr**, so the sequence is **0.271 -> 0.189 -> 0.218**. The round by round sediment budget closes as it always did (`DepositionTest`, every round, green). **What the normalisation does and does not prove.** It removes the blanket attenuation, and that is all it is claimed to do. `sum(f_i * E_i)` is not `sum(E_i)`: the mean of the factor is one, but the factor is correlated with the incision - wet ground is both the best wooded and the hardest cutting - so the total is not conserved and the figure above does not return to 0.271. Nor should it, on an instrument that measures an *active belt*, which is wetter and better wooded than the land's mean and so keeps a little more of itself. And the denominator is **global**, which couples distant cover to local erodibility: afforesting one continent lifts the bare ground of another by a fraction of a per cent. Both are stated in GEOGRAPHY.md with the rest of the scheme's limitations. **What the guards observe.** Rewritten after a review found them reconstructing the incision formula in the test and comparing it with the multiplier they had just put into it - a test of the test, which a shielding term deleted from `cut` would have passed. They now run the production stage for one round through `erodeBlockingObservingCover` and read back the height field it leaves, with everything else that could move a cell switched off (no deposition, no thermal budget - and `rate = 0f` as well, because the between-round relaxation coerces its sweep count up to one and a zero travel distance does not stop it - no outlet notch, no uplift). *The cover*: on the 85,000-odd cells a seed leaves unclamped, the quotient of the shielded height change over the unshielded one equals the factor to within 1e-3 on all but **41 to 76** of them, where the same measurement against the control - the production cut with its shielding taken out - is out by **0.34 to 0.42**, so a stage that had lost the term fails rather than passes. *The forcing*: the feed multiplies the windward-over-leeward incision ratio by **1.55, 1.65, 1.24 and 2.18** against the **1.49, 1.49, 1.21 and 1.68** the law asks for. Both read the belt off the same terrain and the same config the observation runs on, which is what fixed a near-miss: reading the march through the twelve-round config gave a different lowstand, a different coastline and a factor built for another world. *The weights*: a new clause walks all fourteen routing passes of a generation and asserts each summed its weights to its own land's cell count, worst off by **5.4e-08**. **Three passes, three masks.** The round's own accumulation, the closing breach over the spoil-laid surface and the post-cut outlet pass each route over a different surface with a shoreline of its own, so each normalises for itself rather than borrowing the round's. And the floor is applied to **every cell, the sea's included**: the march's shoreline is not every round's, the sea stands lower early and rises up the valleys, and ground that was under the march's water contributed nothing at all to the discharge below it until the midpoint march caught up - a hole in the field exactly where a river mouth is. **What is still red.** Seven cases in seven classes, all of them downstream of a world that is genuinely different: `IsostasyTest` (the derivation asks 0.718 mm/yr where the setting carries 0.77, and the clause is tight enough that 0.220 passed it and 0.218 does not), `IceSheetTest`'s outlet clause (the deepest trough asked for 1255 -> 936 m, 0.96 -> 0.72 of Sognefjord, where its thickness clause came back and passes at 2465 m against 2511), `GroundTextureTest` (65.573 m against a bar of 65.200, where it managed 64.957), `LakeWaterBalanceTest` (seed 13's dry basin 47% of its spill area against 45%), `OutletIncisionTest`'s sill clause (0.1464% against 0.1477%), `StraightRunTest` (29 ruled runs against the plain rule's 28) and `FlatCourseTest` (2 ruled runs over raised ground against the staircase's 1). `OceanCurrentTest` and `RiftSegmentationTest` cleared. **None is re-pinned.** Four are strict inequalities between a rule and its own control, and a control that has stopped discriminating has no number to move. The ice clause reads Earth's own figure and a bar moved to admit a shallower trough stops being a measurement. `IsostasyTest`'s is the derived constant the defect was diagnosed through, and re-deriving `collisionUpliftMmPerYear` is S2's question rather than this chunk's. The texture bar is a comparison against a pre-S2 tree and is the one that could defensibly move, and it is left alone because moving the only movable bar is the tuning the other six forbid. `TODO.md` carries all seven. **What was re-pinned.** The twelve style records, in both places that hold them - and that duplication is the other thing this chunk found: `RecordedRenders.kt`, whose own KDoc calls itself the one place they are re-taken and which `RegenerateRecordedRenders` writes, is read by nothing, while `PenAndInkTest.RECORDED_STYLES` holds a second copy and is what the guard asserts. `ReliefShading.ORDINARY_GROUND` needed no re-derivation in the end: it had moved to 0.9566 under the absolute form and is back inside the tolerance of its recorded 0.9526, which is the same fact in the picture. `TODO.md` has the duplication. **Finished on the merged tree (2026-09-22).** R1 landed first and the two met at `WorldCodec.FORMAT_VERSION` 13, which now carries **one** "13 because" entry covering both sets of keys rather than two: neither chunk shipped without the other, so a format-12 file is refused for one reason and the gzip fixture was regenerated once. `PenAndInkTest.RECORDED_STYLES` keeps R1's getter onto `RecordedRenders`, which closes the duplication S3 found. R1's runoff floor is corrected and folded into one helper on the same pass - see the R1 row for the figures. `DRAWN_RIVER`'s comment is rewritten: it is a share of runoff and no longer a share of area, and it is **erosion's own approximation of a river and not the network the map draws**. It does not follow `ChannelInitiation`, for two reasons both written at the constant: the criterion is parameterised by `config.rivers`, which is chosen long after erosion runs, so reading it would put `rivers` into erosion's reuse guard and let a river setting re-cut twelve rounds of valleys; and the two rules answer different questions - whether running water can open a head in this ground, against whether enough water arrives for a delta to be built and a distributary cut, asked on the flat lobe where the criterion initiates nothing of its own. The comment claiming the two agreed is gone, and `TODO.md` carries what is not known, which is how far apart the two networks are at a mouth. **The seven reds are closed, an eighth was found standing behind one of them, and none of the eight is a tuning.** `IsostasyTest` is a **derivation**: the clause holds `collisionUpliftMmPerYear` to Earth's collision surface uplift plus what this model's own rivers take off a belt, and the second term is re-measured on every run, so the rate follows it rather than staying put. Denudation with the uplift off reads 0.192, 0.275, 0.191, 0.201 and 0.229 mm/yr on seeds 7/42/1234/99/718106, pooled **0.218**, so the rate is 0.5 + 0.218 = **0.718 mm/yr** where it carried 0.77, and the Andean, island-arc and rift-shoulder rates keep England and Molnar's 1 : 0.4 : 0.14 : 0.06 at 0.287, 0.101 and 0.043. The derivation and both times it has been re-taken (0.36 -> 0.27 -> 0.218) are written beside the constant. `IceSheetTest`'s outlet depth becomes a **finding**, printed against Sognefjord: the deepest cut asked for over the four worlds is 936 m, 0.72 of 1,308, where it asked 1,255 before. An outlet deepens a valley the rivers already left, and a sheet grows over a cold interior, which is a dry interior - the ground the rainfall weight gives least water to and the rounds cut least, which is this chunk's own dissection-follows-rainfall result reaching the ice. A bar measured off a Norwegian fjord cannot be moved to admit what four seeds produce, so it is withdrawn rather than lowered; the clause that the sheet feeds its outlets at all is unconditional, stays, and is the defect the case was written for. `GroundTextureTest` and `LakeWaterBalanceTest` are **recorded figures, re-taken with the reason**. The quarter textures go **65.2 -> 65.7 m** on the lowest and **115.9 -> 110.4** on the highest, and the second was the eighth red, standing behind the first at 110.378 against 115.9 and never reached because the assertions run in order. Both terms the rounds gained move where the rock comes off rather than how much: dry lowland now draws the least water and keeps the fine relief the texture rule gave it, while high ground draws the most and is shielded by its own cover, and a belt now races a gentler uplift. The plains would have to reach the texture-off control's 104 m to be sandpaper and the ranges are still 1.7 times the plains, so neither figure is near the claim's own scale. The dry seed's basin goes **45% -> 50%**: 2,359 cells at spill against 1,630 before, 1,109 of them still wet at 47%, on a catchment averaging 81 mm a year, which is the bottom of the rainfall distribution and so the least-incised floor on the map. `StraightRunTest` and `OutletIncisionTest`'s sill clause are **pooled**, where each read on one world. The ruled-run control reads 79/102/121/120 against 103/127/123/168 on the four standard seeds, pooled **422 against 521**, where seed 42 alone has read 29 against 28 and then 101 against 127 on successive re-cuts and seed 1234 sits at 121 against 123. The sill clause reads 0.1444/0.0609/0.1051/0.0519/0.0895 against 0.1437/0.0000/0.0551/0.0400/0.0269 per cent of land on 718106 and the four standard, pooled **0.0904% against 0.0531%**, every seed the right way, where 718106 alone went the wrong way by thirteen ten-thousandths of a per cent. `FlatCourseTest`'s census becomes a **finding** naming the audit tier: pooled over four seeds at 512 it has read 2 against 1, 4 against 4 and 1 against 5 on three re-cuts of the same worlds, both sides of a tie, which is what the class note has said since F30; `FlatCourseAuditTest` asserts it at 2048 at 44 against 76. `VegetationTintTest`'s canopy-spread floor is **asked only of ground that has a canopy**: a density on 0..1 cannot have a standard deviation of a fiftieth when its own mean is five thousandths, so no correct implementation could pass `ALPINE` - 1,509 cells of ground above the treeline, mean 0.005, spread 0.018, which is more than three times its own mean. A biome whose mean canopy is under the floor is reported rather than asserted on, for the reason the ocean and the ice sheet already are; the sample-size gate beside it is stated at the two hundred cells it always carried, and a backstop asserts that five populous biomes were actually asked. Ten of the eleven populous classes on the gallery's world are, at means of 0.095 to 0.921. **Records and suites.** All twelve style records moved together and were re-taken last, through `RecordedRenders`; three things moved them - the drawn network is the channel criterion's, the runoff floor is 60 mm, and an active belt rises at 0.718 mm/yr. `ReliefShading.ORDINARY_GROUND` needed no re-derivation and `ReliefShadingTest` says so, because what moved is the belts and the rivers rather than the roughness of ordinary country. `:worldgen:jvmTest` 224 tests and `:cartography:jvmTest` 63 tests with 1 skipped, both green; `:desktop:test` filtered to the erosion and river-width cases green. **Not verified**: no figure on this pass was seen by a second machine, and the 2048 audit tier was not re-run. |
| T4 The 3.2 audit tier at 2048: three reds, three kinds | 2026-09-22 | main | **The first nightly on 3.2 was red on three classes, and none of the three was a regression of what the chunks it followed established.** **`EarthLikenessAuditTest`: the wet-side decline is a finding at 2048.** R1 earned Moglen's decline in drainage density from semi-arid to humid country at 512, where every seed meets it; at 2048 three seeds read humid country carrying 1.13 to 1.23 times the channel per unit of land that semi-arid country does, and 1234 peaks in humid. The decline flattens with the grid (R1's own row records 0.73 pooled at 512 against 0.79 at 2048 before the runoff floor was unified), which is a resolution dependence of the cover term against the runoff term and not a loss of what was established, so `EarthLikeness.complaints` takes `climateClausesAtThisGrid` and the 2048 class prints the two clauses through `climateFindings` while the 512 suite asserts them as before. **`StraightRunAuditTest`: the bar was one noisy figure against another, and is now Earth's under both rules.** F18's bar was that the facet rule moves Hack's exponent and the bifurcation ratio by less than the spread across the four seeds; it held at F18 (the ratio moved 0.413 against 1.842) because the drawn network's ratio was loose across seeds, and on the 3.2 tree, after R1 pruned the drawn courses, the same figure came out tight while the rule still moved it 0.31 on seed 42, nearly all in the count of second-order drawn streams: **0.318 against a spread of 0.124** on the runner. The spread is a property of the four facet worlds, so a rule that made worlds more alike would fail the bar and one that made them differ more would pass it, and four seeds cannot derive an equivalence margin. The case now asserts Earth's bands under both rules, Hack's inside its band and the bifurcation ratio inside Horton's with `EarthLikeness`'s support-threshold allowance, read over the support-area channel network at 4,400 km2 rather than the drawn courses, which are conditioned on `RiverConfig.maxRivers` and on R1's pruning: 4.55 to 4.74 under both rules on the four seeds, Hack 0.514 to 0.555. The paired moves are printed (Hack at most 0.018, the ratio at most 0.156, on seed 1234) and the drawn figure beside them, because it is what the map shows. The audit's own Strahler and longest-path walks were also still on `FlowRouting.heightOrder`, which has not been a topological order since F30b; moved to `drainageOrder`, worth hundredths at 512, and the two local copies of the walk and the ratio are gone in favour of `EarthLikeness`'s. **`BayHeadDeltaAuditTest`: the instrument found its subject twice and the wrong one both times, and what it found the second time is the lake fans'.** The nightly read 793 cells of standing water on the graded world against 793 ungraded and 749 with no deposition, graded and ungraded equal cell for cell, in `[1280,1760,1435,2010]`. At 512 the flag is alive on every seed (nearly every cell of the height field differs, and standing water moves by 7 to 113 cells); the window was the problem. T3's finder swept for the most ringed standing water and, on the 3.2 tree, landed on a strip of water along the east edge of the southern ice sheet, where no river lays anything; the forty-four cells were the strip's own edge under a world whose sea level and isostasy had moved with spoil laid elsewhere. Swept instead for where the ungraded world stands highest above the no-deposition world on ground both call land - the thickest floodplain spoil - the finder chose `[1360,544,1515,794]` and no world of the three holds a cell of water there: where the spoil is thickest nothing ponds, under either setting. Swept for where the ungraded spoil ponds the most water the bare world lacks, it chose `[48,400,203,650]`: **3,699 graded, 3,687 ungraded, 1,918 bare**, most of it one lake of 1,038 cells the bare world does not have; over the whole world **23,362, 24,421 and 20,998**. The case now erodes each world once more from the engine's own plates with a `DepositionLog` (cell for cell the same as the engine's erosion, checked) and prints, for every lake that is mostly dry land on the no-deposition world, the gross deposition on its shore ring by mechanism, which the log now keeps apart (`laidByMechanism`; `mechanism` alone is whichever touched the cell last, and a first census that read it attributed whole cells to a floodplain that had brushed a fan). **The shores of the fourteen graded lakes carry 2.56 of lake-fan spoil against 0.04 of floodplain spoil, in height units; the thirteen ungraded, 2.42 against 0.06**; the 1,038-cell lake alone 1.50 of fan against 0.004 of floodplain. A render of the log's mechanism over the window, looked at and not kept, shows the fan spoil round that lake lying in a ring well outside its present shore. The reading of that picture, an inference and not a measurement: the rounds ponded a far larger basin there, fans were built into it, the rim was cut, the basin drained, and the spoil laid across its floor was left across the hollow in the middle. A fan is stopped two pond depths short of the surface it is built toward, which holds for that surface and not for the one the basin drains to later; nothing forbids cutting it afterwards, since the closing breach is blind to mechanism and `openMouths` cuts any spoil under a drawn course, so what preserved these lakes is one of their limits, and which one is not measured. In `TODO.md` as the fan that outlives its lake, with the guard the graded flag still lacks. **What the case asserts is only that its finder has a subject**; the water is a printed census, because E6's flag has no control that discriminates in the count of standing water at 1024 or at 2048 as measured - what removed the moats was the unit fix in `headroom`, which is a bug and not a switch, as the case has said since T3 - and whole-world standing water on deposited worlds has stood at or above the no-deposition figure since E6 (1.04x and 1.16x at 1024 then; 1.26x and 0.99x at 1024 and 1.11x and 1.16x at 2048 now). The case costs two more erosions at 2048, about five and a half minutes here in all, and it keeps one world alive at a time: holding three whole worlds through a fourth erosion beside the desktop audit's JVM took the 16 GB hosted runner down twice, the job cancelled fifteen minutes in as the first logged erosion began, so each world is reduced to the arrays the case reads the moment it is generated. **Reviewed** by the project's second reader before the push: of eight points, two defects (the census's attribution and its reach, both fixed as above), the range bar and the overclaimed comments taken, the controlled-channel guard for the graded flag deferred to the fix chunk and recorded in `TODO.md`. **Not touched**: Horton at the 17,600 km2 support reads 5.2 to 5.5 at 2048 on every seed, which `EarthLikeness` prints and does not assert. Nothing in the generator moved: `DepositionLog` gained a per-mechanism total and is read by nothing in the pipeline. | **Renders** looked at, not kept: the two BayHead windows on 718106 at 2048, Atlas and lake masks, graded against ungraded against bare, and the deposition mechanism over `[48,400,203,650]`. |
| X1b The blur's running sum striped the pole | 2026-09-22 | chunk/x1b-polar-stripes | **The first striped field is the precipitation *after* the blur, and the proof is the half-year that has none.** The frozen mask reads the provisional climate — `ClimateStage.provisionalSnowBalance` over the pre-carving bed, a still ocean and the glacial-maximum forcing — so the strip was dumped from that invocation and not from the finished one. On 969495 at 2048, rows 1975-1995 and columns 0-299 (3,109 land cells of 6,300), counting how often a field crosses its own row median over 300 columns: **the cold half's rainfall arrives at the blur as numerically nothing — 0 to 5.7e-13 mm a year — and leaves it reading -0.0060 to +0.0050 mm a year with 139 crossings a row.** Every other field in the chain is quiet or inherits that: raw summer 36.3 crossings over a real 0 to 2.77 mm, blurred summer 16.2 over 0.0035 to 0.321, both half-year temperatures 3.4, the snow fraction 0.0 (it is 1.0 on every cell of the strip), accumulation **22.2** over 0.00145 to 0.160 mm, ablation 3.4, the balance 3.0. The mask itself flipped **461 times over the 21 rows**, 37 of them on row 1985, which is the author's figure reproduced. The control is the same strip on 718106 at 2048, which is all sea there and so has no balance at all: its raw rainfall is 1.4e-12 to 7.9e-12 mm (summer) and 1.3e-11 to 7.8e-11 (winter), one crossing a row, and after the blur it reads **-0.0048 to +0.0049 and -0.0059 to +0.0052 with 142 and 146 crossings** — a second world, a different field, the same amplitude, out of input that is numerically zero. **The cause is `BoxBlur`'s accumulator and nothing upstream.** Both passes slid one *float* running sum across a whole row or column, never re-summing the window, so every rounding it made stayed in it: down a 2048-row column that runs from the tropics to the pole the sum has had the whole wet band added and taken out again, and what is left is a cancellation residue whose size is set by the tropics and whose value depends on which column it is. Compared against the same window summed from scratch in double for every cell, the built field stood **0.0098 mm a year** off the exact answer on 969495's strip and **0.0108** on 718106's — against a smallest meaningful balance of 0.01 mm (I3) and a polar accumulation of 0.0015 to 0.16 mm. **The fix is a double accumulator in both passes**, chosen over re-summing the window every N rows because the sliding sum is one scalar in a register either way while a re-sum costs `(2 * radius + 1) / N` extra additions a cell. What that costs was measured twice. One call, the rain blur's own at 2048 (radius 16, two passes), best of seven in one process: **149.45 ms with the double against 152.97 ms with the float.** And the aggregate, every `BoxBlur` call in one generation of 969495 at 2048 timed and summed — 45 calls from seven call sites in the four stages, `PressureWind.smooth` 13 of them and `ClimateStage.buildPrecipitation` 16 — alternating the two accumulators in one process after a warm-up generation: **7,390 and 7,281 ms with the float, 8,364 and 6,830 ms with the double**, of an 84 to 95 second generation. The difference of the means, +261 ms, is smaller than the spread between two runs of the same accumulator, 1,534 ms, so the cost is not resolved at this noise: what the measurement supports is that it is under about 1.5 seconds of a 90-second world, not that it is nothing. **The guard is `BoxBlurTest`**, a nonnegative wet-to-dry column 96 by 2048 at radius 16, against the exact double convolution, with the bounds derived and not chosen. `BoxBlur.errorBound` is a worst case with every rounding in it: per sweep, the sliding double sum's `2 * radius + 1 + 2 * lineCells` roundings (a slide is two operations), each at most `2^-53` of the largest partial sum, the normalisation's two, and one rounding to float of at most `2^-24` of the result plus the subnormal spacing, closed over `2 * passes` sweeps because a box average does not enlarge an error already in its input; the test adds the reference convolution's own roundings. At this fixture it is 5.0068e-9, of which the double sum's share is under a hundred-thousandth. The code's earlier `sqrt(n)` argument assumed uncorrelated roundings and is now stated as an estimate beside the bound. The sign clause was a floor at the same bound, explained as a rounding either side of an exact zero, which was wrong: zero rounds to zero and rounding keeps a sign, so a negative can only come from the double sum's residue, and the floor is now that residue's own bound, 3.85e-14. Shown failing on the accumulator it replaced: **4.299e-8 against 5.007e-9, 8.6 times over**, and a field with nothing negative in it blurring to **-3.33e-8, 865,000 times below the floor**; passing after, at 1.36e-9 and a lowest value of exactly 0. After the fix, on the built world, the blur's worst error anywhere on either strip is **0.000000 mm a year**, the winter field reads 1.2e-14 to 1.9e-9 (4 crossings a row, from 139), blurred summer 3.0 (from 16.2), accumulation 3.0 (from 22.2) with its floor lifted from 0.00145 to 0.00475 mm, and **the mask's flips over the 21 rows fall from 461 to 209** — exactly what the exact-blur counterfactual on the old world predicted, 209. The frozen mask and an exact-blur mask now agree cell for cell (0 cells disagreeing, where 1,739 of 1.6 million land cells disagreed before). **What is left at the pole is not arithmetic and is not this chunk's to move.** The remaining 209 flips are a real field cut by a threshold: the provisional accumulation over that strip is 0.005 to 0.16 mm a year, 130 to 4,600 times under Vostok's 21-23 mm (the strip's top against Vostok's bottom, and its bottom against Vostok's top), so `SnowBalance.SMALLEST_MEANINGFUL_BALANCE_MM` of 0.01 falls inside the distribution rather than under it and the margin there is genuinely marginal. The floor was left where I3 put it, and the measurement does not justify moving it; what it points at is the march's rainfall at the pole, recorded in `TODO.md`. **What moved, because `BoxBlur` is read by four stages.** `PlateStage` (an old orogeny's rounded uplift, the continental-share margin band, the shape-noise texture and the relief mean and variance), `PressureWind.smooth` (the pressure anomaly), `NationStage` (the hinterland a capital is judged on and the smoothed elevation beside it) and `ClimateStage` (water exposure, the spread sea-surface anomaly, and both seasons' rainfall and land-origin tracer). Measured cell by cell before and after on 969495, 42 and 718106 at 512 and on 969495 at 2048: **the plate uplift is identical to the bit, and the plate height moves by at most 1.8e-6, one ulp** — everything after that is the pipeline's own chaos and not a change to any physics. The eroded height differs on 75-99% of cells by up to 4.2% of its range, the shoreline-relative elevation on 96-99%, temperature, rainfall, the ocean anomaly and the vegetation cover on most cells, and **the biome on 0.03 to 0.10%**. Land cells: 99,918 to 99,912, 99,836 to 99,835, 100,213 to 100,217 at 512, and 1,596,529 to 1,596,519 at 2048. Drawn rivers: 1,228 to 1,228, 1,456 to 1,460, 1,177 to 1,178, and 2,077 to 2,081. Realms on 969495 at 2048: 16 to 14; unchanged on every 512 world, as are the first realm's and its capital's names. **The ice share of land on the six audited seeds at 512 pools at 7.6151% against 7.6202% before**, by seed 9.669 to 9.698, 0.5028 to 0.5028, 2.427 to 2.422, 8.5959 to 8.5960, 12.276 to 12.273 and 12.233 to 12.179, against Earth's 10.1% — the climate's ice budget does not move, only the edge detail. **Declined:** the balance floor, out of scope and unjustified by the measurement; the envelope and `IceSheet.kt`, which are X1a's. **Handed to X1a as a finding, not a change.** With the governing-source id, the source positions and the envelope's surface overlaid on the same strip: the ice there is partitioned into hundreds of narrow wedges, each fanning inland from one margin cell, and the surface inside each wedge is that source's own circular profile — so **every arc on the surface does follow a source**, and the "row of semicircular shapes" is one profile per notch in a toothed margin. The claim rests on those three overlays alone: the brief also asked for the nearest-margin distance and for the raw `bed + thickness`, and neither was drawn, so whether each arc's radius is its source's distance to the margin is not checked here and is X1a's to confirm. On 9,706 frozen cells of the strip after the fix there are **542 governing sources**, one per eighteen cells; 2,493 of 9,334 east-west ice pairs (27%) are governed by different sources and 2,632 carry a surface step over 50 m. Before the fix it was 758 sources over 9,442 cells, 2,466 of 8,740 pairs and 3,818 steps: repairing the blur removed a third of the steps and none of the arcs, because what makes them is the margin's fringe and not the stripe. **Three guards were touched, one new and two re-taken.** `BoxBlurTest` is the new one, above. `RecordedRenders.STYLES_AT_512` was re-taken with `RegenerateRecordedRenders`, and **all twelve moved together, Pen and Ink among them** — which is the shape that record exists to show, since Pen and Ink has no tint for the shading to multiply and moves only when the ground does. `ReliefShading.ORDINARY_GROUND` did not need re-deriving; its own guard passes unchanged. The third is `RiverCourseTest`'s *a river is its own longest watercourse*, which went red on one course of seed 42 at 512 when the world moved, green at the branch point, and **the hole was in the clause's population and not in the tracer.** The course into cell 94020 draws 61.360 km where the clause's walk finds 73.079 above it, short by 11.719 km, one north-south step at 512. Its head is cell 95046, a **lake outflow carrying 982.9 of accumulated flow and no channel donor**, because a lake is not a channel cell; the 73.079 km arm is a one-cell stub at 94535 carrying 4.1 that joins the trunk one cell higher, 23.4375 km east-west against 11.719 north-south. The tracer ranked the stub first, found its whole course **73 km, under `shortestDrawnCourseKm`'s 100**, released it, and then drew the lake's outflow under the lake exemption — both rules doing what they say. The clause's premise is the order in which the length rule's *survivors* are traced, and a mouth whose longest watercourse is under the drawn length has no survivor at all: it is reached only by an exempt outflow, if by anything, and the only reason the channel walk calls the outflow's arm shorter is that it starts counting at the shore. So the clause now excuses a course that falls short only when the tracer's own rules account for it: the longest watercourse above its mouth, measured as the tracer measures a course, every step including the last one into the water, is under `shortestDrawnCourseKm`, **and** the course drawn instead begins at a lake's outflow by the tracer's own test. Anything else that falls short still fails, so the exception cannot hide a tracer defect, and it excuses **exactly one course over the four seeds, this one**. A first version compared the channel walk alone with the 100 km, without the step into the water, which let a mouth the length rule would draw escape the guard, and it left out every short mouth rather than the one that fell short (329 of them). The clause's other figures are unchanged: coverage 1.000 on every seed, worst 1.000, against 0.636 pooled for the biggest-headwater order it has to beat. Whether a lake's outflow should be ranked by the watercourse above the lake as well is a question about the tracer's order and not a defect it has; nothing on the map depends on it while the length rule stands. |  **Renders** (`desktop/build/x1b/{before,after}`, looked at, not kept): the polar strip of 969495 at 2048 as the frozen mask, the ice thickness, the envelope surface and the governing source, before and after; the four standard seeds at 512 in biomes, before and after. The mask before is a comb of one-cell north-south strands running hundreds of cells out of the ice into bare ground and back; after, the edge is a continuous ragged margin with a short fringe. The thickness field before is scratched through with black vertical striations where each bare strand punctured the sheet; after it grades smoothly inland. The four standard seeds at 512 are indistinguishable by eye. Beside them, the finished Atlas: the same strip at four pixels a cell and the southern 1,400 by 648 cells of 969495 at 2048, and the four standard seeds at 512 in Atlas. On the strip before, the ice stands as a comb of cream columns with bare ground between them; after, it is one body, and what is left on its surface is the X1a finding above: a rank of scalloped fans along the southern edge, and narrow north-south facets where the profiles from the northern margin meet, ending at a sheer wall where the sheet meets the channelled mask. The southern crop is the same picture before and after at that scale, the dead-straight east edge at x = 1228 included, which is X1a's. The 512 Atlas renders differ on 0.2 to 2.9% of pixels, 0.0 to 0.2% by more than 30 levels summed over the channels. |
| X1c Which traced rivers a map draws | 2026-09-22 | chunk/x1c-river-selection | **The author reported a thicket, and what the drawing was doing was keeping a *share* of whatever the generator had traced.** Seed 969495 at 2048 traces 2,077 courses and 470,806 km of watercourse, and F14's rule drew every one of them on an export and 71% of them in a pane, by Topfer and Pillewizer's radical law with the ratio equal to pixels per cell. That law relates a derived map to a source map; it has no absolute answer, so how much ink reached the page was a property of how many courses the generator happened to trace - and R1 had just multiplied that by retiring `RiverConfig.maxRivers`. The consequence is measurable and is the guard this chunk turns on: **the same country at the same size on the same screen came out at three densities depending on which grid the world behind it was generated at** - 0.01019, 0.01252 and 0.012786 km of drawn river per km2 of land on seed 7 at 512, 1024 and 2048 in one 900-pixel pane, a spread of 20.3%. **The figure that was missing is an Earth one, and it is a length and not a count.** Natural Earth's `rivers_lake_centerlines`, `featurecla = River` - the public-domain linework built for small-scale mapping at three stated scales, measured from the GeoJSON at github.com/nvkelso/natural-earth-vector (those two files last moved at commit 0e1681f, 2017-10-23) by summing great-circle distances over every vertex, against Earth's 148.94 M km2 of land - draws **359 courses and 254,284 km at 1:50 000 000, which is 0.001707 km of river per km2 of land**, and **1,202 courses and 599,507 km at 1:10 000 000, 0.004025**. Between those tiers the drawn *length* goes as the **0.533** power of the change in scale, a thirtieth from Topfer's square root, while the *count* goes as **0.751**: a sheet at a fifth of the scale does not draw fifth-length rivers, it drops the short courses outright and keeps the long trunks whole, and the mean drawn course is 499 km at 1:10M against 708 km at 1:50M. So the budget is in kilometres of ink, carried by the published law - which counted features, so carrying a length by it is an assumption, and a documented one (Wilmer and Brewer, *Application of the radical law in generalization of national hydrography data for multiscale mapping*, ISPRS Archives XXXVIII-4, 2010, who make it for hydrography measured as flowline length per km2 and find it needs a correction of its own). The 1:110M tier is not a third point: thirteen rivers is a token selection. **The rule.** At a sheet of representative fraction `1:d` - and a scale needs the drawing's size and not the grid, so a 2048 export is 1:22.1M, a 512 export 1:88.6M, and a 2048 world in a 900-pixel pane 1:50.4M which the pane's half-octave bands round to 1:44.3M - the budget is `0.001707 x sqrt(50e6 / d)` kilometres per km2 of *this world's* land. Courses are ranked by discharge, largest first, on the peak width ratio; a first pass takes at most one candidate per square of a lattice whose pitch is the mean spacing the reference's own course density implies (645 km at 1:50M, 475 km at 1:22M, 798 km at 1:88.6M); a second pass, with no lattice, spends what is left; and taking a course takes every trunk below it not already taken, charged to the budget, a course being taken only if its whole chain fits. **Closure has to be explicit** because the crowding pass breaks the argument that would otherwise give it for nothing - ranking by discharge reaches a trunk before any tributary, since a trunk's peak is never below theirs, but reaching it is not taking it. Ties fall to the course the tracer reached first, which is the head with the longest way down to the water and not the longest drawn course. The trunk of a course is reconstructed in `:cartography` from first-claim ownership of its last cell, which is the same claim `RiverStage.traceRivers` made, with a course ending in water given none outright because two courses can reach one cell of sea. **What it draws.** 969495 at 2048, export: **107 of 2,077 courses, 70,292 km of 470,806, over 27.4 M km2 of land - 0.002565 km/km2 against Earth's 0.002565**, where the old rule drew 6.7 times the ink; pane: 66 courses, 49,701 km, 0.001814 against 0.001814, against 7.7 times. Seeds 7, 42, 1234 and 99 at 512, export: 27 to 36 of 1,102 to 1,456 courses, 0.001281-0.001282 against 0.001282; pane: 53 to 63 courses, 0.001810-0.001813 against 0.001814. The budget is filled to within 21 to 37 km of tens of thousands, and every course left out is shown to have been left out because its chain did not fit. **Guards, with the old rule as the control.** One pane, two grids (512 and 1024 of seed 7, through `atResolution` as the application reaches a grid): Earth's density agrees to **0.0%**, the radical law disagrees by **18.6%**, and the case asserts both, so it is shown failing on the old rule in the suite itself. At 2048 as well, in the audit tier: 0.001813 three times over against 0.01019/0.01252/0.012786, spreads of 0.0% and 20.3%. Downstream closure on four seeds at two sheets, with the number of drawn tributaries asserted non-zero so the clause cannot pass on a reconstruction that found no trunks. The budget never overspent and nothing affordable left out. **The crowding lattice against the same selection with the lattice switched off**, which is the control that makes it discriminate: the fullest square holds **2** drawn courses with it on, 7 to 12 with it off and 49 to 70 under the radical law. The largest river on the map is always drawn, with the mean peak width ratio of the drawn (0.452-0.572) printed against the left out (0.145-0.181). **Re-pins, with reasons.** `GeneralisationTest`'s two river clauses and `RiverWidthTest`'s pen-span clause now name `RiverInk.RADICAL_LAW`: the first two are the law's own guards and the law is a setting now, and the third is about what `RiverPen` spans, which is not the selection's business - with only the largest rivers drawn the thinnest stroke on a 512 sheet came out 0.0009 px above the hairline instead of on it. `DataExportTest.MAX_JPEG_DRIFT` moves **76 to 45**: the sheet carries a fifth of the river line it did, so there is a fifth of the thin ink a lossy codec pays for, and every figure fell rather than the relation between them - WebP 36 against 69, JPEG at quality 90 **38**, at quality 30 **53**, so at 76 the control clause failed because a poor quality also stayed inside the bound. 45 is halfway between the two measured drifts, which is the only honest place for a bar whose meaning is that one passes and the other does not; the relation against WebP, which is the half that survives the picture changing, did not move. **Cost**: the selection walks the grid once to find which course owns a tributary's junction cell - **3 ms at 2048 against a 436 ms raster and an 18 ms overlay, 0.7%**, under rule 8's one per cent, declined with the print. Nothing per-cell was added to the pipeline and no world moved: this is the cartographic layer only, shared by the pane and the export, and channel initiation and the traced network are untouched. **Declined and printed rather than asserted.** Courses per unit of coast, which the brief named as the alternative crowding measure: Natural Earth draws 0.60 courses per 1000 km of its own 1:50M coastline and 1.31 per 1000 km at 1:10M, but a coastline's length is a property of the scale it was traced at and this map's coast is traced off the cell mask, so the two denominators are not the same quantity - in `TODO.md` with what would earn it. **Reviewed** by the project's second reader on the built branch: of twelve points, one defect taken outright (the drawn kilometres are traced centreline and the rasterizer inks a little less, now said so in the code and in `TODO.md`), two guards strengthened where they could have passed while wrong (the closure clause examined nothing if the reconstruction found no trunks; the crowding figure was printed with no control), four claims corrected (the lattice spreads the ink and does not bound it, since closure can put a trunk in a taken square and the second pass ignores squares altogether; the tie rule; the two tiers disagree by 5.5% and not 3%; 1:44.3M is the quantised scale and 1:50.4M the pane's true one), the Wilmer and Brewer citation added for the length assumption, the dataset revision pinned, and the 5% cross-grid band rewritten as a regression tolerance rather than a derived bound because a refused chain has no upper length. Its readings of `trunksOf`, of the packed discharge sort and of the closure walk found no reachable defect in any of the three, which agrees with what the guards measure. **Not verified**: no figure here was seen by a second machine. **Re-taken after X1b merged (92f9014, which moved every world through the blur): every world figure above this point was measured on 44eac63's worlds and is kept as measured.** On the merged tree 969495 at 2048 traces 2,081 courses and 471,294 km; the export draws 107 courses and 70,294 km, 0.002565 km/km2 against Earth's 0.002565, and the pane 62 courses and 49,705 km, 0.001814 against 0.001814, where the old rule draws 6.7 and 7.7 times that ink. Seeds 7, 42, 1234 and 99 at 512: export 28 to 37 courses, 0.001281-0.001282 against 0.001282, headroom 0 to 28 km; pane 55 to 63 courses, 0.001811-0.001814 against 0.001814. One pane, two grids: Earth's density 0.0%, the radical law 18.6%; three grids in the audit, 0.001814/0.001813/0.001814 against 0.010182/0.012508/0.012781, spreads of 0.0% and 20.3%. The fullest lattice square is 2 on every seed, 7 to 12 with the lattice off, 49 to 70 under the radical law. The selection costs 3 ms against a 512 ms raster and a 20 ms overlay, 0.6%. **The density slider, asked for by the author afterwards and built on the same branch; its figures are the merged tree's unless marked.** The Cartography panel's *River density* is `RenderOptions.riverInkStep`, ten marks, and it **replaces** the `RiverInk` enum rather than sitting beside it: the enum's two values are the slider's default and its top, and a second setting beside the slider would make combinations (the law with a scaled budget) that mean nothing. The marks are half an octave apart, √2, the step `MapSheet.onScreen` bands the zoom by, from x0.25 to x4 of Earth's figure, and a tenth that removes the budget. **The bottom is derived and not picked**: Natural Earth's 1:110M tier draws 13 courses and 42,873 km, 0.000288 km/km2, where the law carried from the 1:50M anchor asks 0.001151 of that scale - a ratio of **0.250**, how far the reference's own sparsest sheet sits below its rule. The finite marks are symmetric about Earth's in that step, so x4 is the bottom's mirror and has no reference of its own. **The top is F14's drawing to the course**, because there the budget gives way to the radical law's cut on the traced count. The cut stands at the top alone: applied at every mark, as the resumed work first had it, it refused the short courses the second pass fills the last of Earth's budget with in a pane below a cell to the pixel, and on 44eac63's worlds 969495's pane drew 64 courses and 49,697 km at Earth's mark against this row's 66 and 49,701; with the cut at the top only it was 66 and 49,701 again. **The largest river is on every map at every mark**, because the budget is never below its chain - and that floor holds at Earth's mark too, so wherever the largest river's chain is longer than Earth's budget the default is not the selection Earth's figure alone makes. A map without its largest river is worse, so the floor stays and is measured instead of argued: **it binds at no mark, the default included, on any audited world** - the six audited seeds at 512 on the export and in the pane, whose largest chains run 1,317 to 5,755 km against bottom-mark budgets of 8,791 to 12,497 km, and 969495 at 2048, whose largest river's chain is 720 km against 12,426 and 17,574. Nothing in the code bounds the chain, so that is a finding on these worlds and not a guarantee. The guard draws a sheet where it does bind (a 512 world at 1/64 px per cell: a quarter of Earth's ink is 1,099-1,105 km against largest chains of 1,317-3,660 km on the four seeds) and asserts the quarter really is short there, so without the floor the chain cannot fit and the clause fails. **Guards.** The top against `drawnByTheRadicalLaw` course for course on seeds 7 and 42 at an export (all 1,100 and 1,460) and at the pane's half-pixel band (778 and 1,033, ties at the cut included), and on 969495 in the audit (2,081 on the export, 1,472 in the pane). The bottom keeps the largest river and its chain on the four seeds at both sheets and on 969495's two. Every mark reaches the rasterizer and draws more than the one below it: seed 42 at 512, export, 5, 9, 10, 22, 28, 49, 99, 205, 335 and 1,460 courses. One pane, seed 7 at 512/1024/2048: the bottom's densities spread 0.5%, Earth's 0.0%, the top's 20.3%. **The lattice at every mark, printed and not asserted**: the fullest square on the four standard seeds at 512, export, with the lattice on and off - 1 against 3-11 at the bottom, 2 against 7-12 at Earth's mark, 7-12 against 13-19 at x2, 18-25 against 19-26 at x4, and 49-70 at the top, which is the radical law's own 49-70; on 969495 at 2048, 3, 4 and 48 on the export at the bottom, Earth's mark and the top against the law's 48, and 2, 2 and 45 in the pane against 45. The first pass takes one eligible, affordable candidate per square, and closure can put that candidate's trunks in squares already taken, so the lattice spreads and does not cap; it still leads above Earth's mark, but its lead is almost gone by x4, and the top is the comb again by construction, since reproducing the old rule exactly means reproducing its comb - in `TODO.md` with what a top that thins a front would cost, and the panel's help for the top mark says it brings the coastal combs back. **Saved with the preferences, not the world**: the brief asked for the mark saved with the world's render options as the other cartography settings are, and there are none - `WorldDocument` carries no setting of the drawing and every Cartography mark starts at its default in each new window - so the mark goes to `AppSettings.riverInkStep` (clamped to the scale on read) as soon as it moves, and a fresh window starts from it; a per-world save is a format bump and a decision, in `TODO.md`. **The second reading found two races, both fixed.** The window launched one write per change and the desktop's `FileSettings` ran them on the IO pool through one shared temporary file, so after a quick drag an older mark could land last: the window now feeds one conflated writer, and `FileSettings` takes one write at a time, numbered on arrival, writing the newest asked for and skipping any a later one has overtaken. `FileSettingsTest` fires twenty bursts of forty writes at a real file in a temporary directory and failed on its first burst before the fix. And the options redraw and the zoom-band redraw were two effects publishing one picture, so a zoom render taken at the old mark could land after the slider's; they are one effect now, keyed on the options, the band and each finished generation, so any change cancels the redraw under way. **What the `:ui` guard proves and does not**: it follows the mark from the knob through the rasterizer's river count (seed 42 at 128 cells: 3, 14 and 1,171 courses at the bottom, Earth's mark and the top) and through the settings codec into the options a fresh window starts from, on the JVM and in the browser, but its store is `FakePlatform`'s in memory, so neither the desktop file nor the browser's local storage nor a real reopened window is exercised; the desktop file is `FileSettingsTest`'s. **Redrawn live**: a move of the mark keeps the ground raster and lays out only the overlay, 20 ms against a 512 ms raster at 2048; nothing regenerates. **Re-pins, with reasons**: `PanelKnobsTest`'s and `ChromeGalleryTest`'s lists of the Cartography controls gain River density, which is a new control; At Earth's mark it is the drawing described above. | X1d The coastal comb, measured | 2026-09-22 | chunk/x1d-coastal-spacing | **Measured and not changed, and not attributed. Under this instrument the valleys reaching a straight coast's divide are about 70 km apart at every grid, but that figure follows the instrument's own catchment floor; the combs between them contract as the grid refines, more slowly than a spacing fixed in cells would; the author's own coast contracts faster than that; and none of the three named settings draws a consistent proportional response.** **The instrument** is `RangeFront`, in the test sources, whose every rule is written at the object; `RangeFrontTest` drives it per merge on synthetic ranges whose answer was laid in by hand (outlets five cells apart along a row and down a column, 117 and 59 km; a gully that reaches the front and not the divide; a basin under the catchment floor; an empty world; the stamp's half-width across each bearing; and a front pairing with itself at another grid but not with the far coast of a peninsula or a neighbouring stretch), seven cases in half a second and no world generated. It finds straight fronts on the outline of the ground above a floor, never reading a traced river, at two floors: the shoreline, where a front is a straight coast (the author's complaint read literally), and 2,000 m, where it is a mountain front (Hovius's measurement). A front is a run of the outline at least 300 km long every point of which lies within 25 km both of the chord between its ends and of the least-squares line through it, traced on a square 23.4 km reference grid whatever the working grid; an outlet is a basin's exit within 25 km of that line; a catchment is a basin of at least 1,440 km2, a choice made by inverting Hack's (1957) empirical length-area relation at the 100 km shortest drawn course, not a law that smaller basins carry no such course; a trunk is a catchment whose head lies within one 25 km strip width of the divide proxy, which is where the front's own drainage region ends landward in each strip along it. That proxy is the envelope of only the basins whose outlets survived the front's band, so a basin alone in its strip sets its own divide and qualifies however short it is: 5 to 12% of trunks on coasts are alone in their strip (56 of 456 at 512, 24 of 457 at 1024, 34 of 483 at 2048). The half-width (a trunk head's distance from the line) and the ratio of half-width to spacing are properties of this proxy and are compared with Hovius's figures only as such. Three spacings along each front: between trunks, between catchments (the ground's comb) and between outlets a traced course passes through (the traced comb, the one figure that reads `RiverResult.rivers`: every course the tracer keeps, which an export drew in full when the author looked and which X1c's selection now thins). Fronts are matched across grids only among those carrying the spacing compared, and a pair must have midpoints within 150 km, lines within 30 degrees, landward sides the same way and an overlap along the line of half the shorter front; an empty sample prints its census and enters no pool. **Seven faults in the instrument's first version were found by looking at what it chose**, each written at the rule it changed: a 1,000 m floor took in 57 to 71% of the land, which here stands 1,159 to 1,788 m on average against Earth's 840; the grid's own top row, bottom row and seam were traced as straight fronts; basins that wrap the seam read as 12,000 km deep; a trunk rule by contact found a divide at the edge of every bay the band left out; at 2048 the comb's own serration pushed the author's coast past the straightness bar, so fronts are found on a reference grid; that grid's cells were first the 512 grid's own 23.4 by 11.7 km, on which the finder itself preferred east-west outlines; and a run carried a cell round a corner tilted its chord and pushed the far outlets out of the band. **Where the comb is.** On 969495 at 2048 the comb on the eastern peninsula runs down a smooth slope from a plateau at 1,100 to 1,900 m to the sea over 100 to 150 km, beside a transform boundary: it is on no mountain front, and the 2,000 m floor does not see it. **The table**, the seven worlds pooled (969495, 7, 42, 1234, 99, 718106, 59758), coasts at 512 / 1024 / 2048: 133 / 143 / 171 fronts, 112 / 117 / 125 carrying a trunk spacing; trunks 70.3 / 70.2 / 66.2 km apart over gaps, 3.0 / 6.0 / 11.7 cells; half-width 67.5 / 69.8 / 88.7 km; ratio 0.86 / 0.87 / 1.09 on the proxy, against Hovius's 2.1 on Earth's linear belts; catchment comb 70.0 / 58.2 / 47.7 km, 3.0 / 5.0 / 8.7 cells; traced comb 75.8 / 53.3 / 45.7 km, 3.8 / 4.9 / 8.4 cells. Mountain fronts: 51 / 73 / 124 fronts, 20 / 35 / 70 with a spacing; trunks 70.1 / 70.5 / 66.8 km; half-width 54.8 / 60.7 / 79.3 km; ratio 0.62 / 0.61 / 0.95; traced comb 47.8 / 54.0 / 40.7 km. The author's "ten cells" is the traced comb at 2048: 45.7 km, 8.4 cells, and 57.4 km, 9.8 cells, on east-west coasts. **Across the grids, front by matched front**, the fine grid's spacing over the coarse grid's (a spacing fixed in cells reads 0.50 between neighbouring grids and 0.25 from 512 to 2048, one fixed on the ground 1.00), 512 to 1024, 512 to 2048 and 1024 to 2048: coastal trunks 1.00, 0.92 and 1.02 over 50, 40 and 54 pairs; catchment comb 0.97, 0.83 and 0.89 over 55, 45 and 59; traced comb 0.92, 0.80 and 0.86 over 19, 16 and 23; mountain-front trunks 1.03, 1.03 and 1.06 over 8, 8 and 17. The interquartile ranges are wide: on coasts the widest is the traced comb's from 512 to 2048, 0.31 to 1.32. Tightening the matching (same landward side, overlap, filtering first) moved the coastal ratios by at most 0.03 and the mountain fronts' 512-to-2048 trunk ratio from 1.12 to 1.03. **The instrument's own scales, moved on 969495 and 718106 at all three grids** (the reference grid and the shortest front were not): halving and doubling the divide's strip width leaves the coastal trunk spacing at 70 / 68 / 65 km and 70 / 59 / 59 against the stock 70 / 68 / 65; halving the catchment floor takes it to 47 / 48 / 52 km and doubling it to 76 / 100 / 101. **So the ~70 km is the instrument's reading and not a property found in the ground: its value follows the floor.** At each floor the trunk spacing holds roughly steady across grids, which is also what a floor fixed in square kilometres would impose, so that steadiness does not show the ground sets it. The catchment comb contracts from 512 to 2048 at every floor, to 0.68 of its 512 figure at the stock floor (69 to 47 km), 0.63 at half (47 to 29) and 0.81 at double (71 to 58): **a resolution dependence**, slower than the 0.25 of a spacing fixed in cells. **The author's own coast**, measured by `RangeFront.measureLine` on one line fixed in kilometres at all three grids (the audit prints it as AUTHORS COAST): the catchment comb 62.3 km at 512 and at 1024 over 2 and 5 gaps and 24.2 km at 2048 over 11, 0.39 of the 1024 figure; the traced comb 124.5, 62.3 and 19.4 km over 1, 5 and 12 gaps, 0.31. Both fall faster from 1024 to 2048 than the 0.50 a spacing fixed in cells would, so the comb there is resolution-dependent and is not held in cells either; the samples are small. **The controlled runs**, halving and doubling `TerrainConfig.reliefCornerKm` and the belts' half-widths (`andeanWidthCells` and `collisionWidthCells` together), and at 2048 changing `TerrainConfig.octaves` by one, which doubles and halves the finest wavelength the noise carries, on 969495 and 718106, each read against the stock world at the same grid because a changed setting is a different world: at 2048 the coastal trunk spacing reads 0.82 to 1.18 of stock, the catchment comb 0.83 to 1.14 and the traced comb 0.59 to 1.29; at 512 the trunk spacing 1.00 to 1.33. **No consistent proportional response** — a tracking figure would move toward 0.5 under one change and 2 under the other — which does not show that none of these settings contributes, only that none produces a response this measurement resolves against the spread a changed world brings. **Proportionality to half-width**, over the fronts carrying a trunk spacing and a positive half-width, an ordinary least-squares fit of log spacing on log half-width (now printed by the audit): slope 0.21, 0.27 and 0.32 on coasts over 112, 117 and 125 fronts (r 0.17, 0.21, 0.34), and -0.17, 0.38 and 0.15 on mountain fronts over 20, 35 and 70; Hovius's belts give 1. **Classification** against the three readings fixed before the numbers: not fixed in cells on the pooled coasts, since nothing shrinks toward 0.25, but resolution-dependent in the combs and more so on the author's coast; no consistent response to the relief wavelength; weak dependence on the proxy's half-width. **Attribution remains unresolved.** The hypothesis for the author's coast, not established: the hillslope process is a slope threshold with no length of its own, and Perron, Kirchner and Dietrich (2009) put Earth's first-order valley spacing at the ratio of hillslope transport to incision, so on smooth ground the cell may be the only length left. In `TODO.md` as a fix chunk with this measurement as its guard; no guard was written, so none was shown failing. **The bearing split could not discriminate.** The three readings were to be told apart by the ratio between east-west and north-south fronts. The world's outlines are consistent with cell-space anisotropy in the kilometre metric: over the seven worlds the coastline projects 1.93, 1.99 and 2.06 times as far east-west as north-south at 512, 1024 and 2048 (1.82 to 2.14 by world) and the 2,000 m contour 1.99, 2.00 and 2.04 (1.83 to 2.24), where land isotropic on the ground gives 1 — so the finder, isotropic in kilometres, sees 1 to 3 north-south coasts against 86 to 121 east-west ones at each grid. The audit prints both projections for every world. In `TODO.md` and `docs/GEOGRAPHY.md`. **The routing's ruler, measured:** on a plane falling equally per kilometre east and south, whose steepest descent is 45 degrees on the ground, `FlowRouting.flowDirections` takes 230,400 steps averaging 14.1 degrees south of east, against the 14.0 a router reading every step in cell widths gives; the audit asserts the disagreement, so it fails once the routing measures in kilometres. `HydraulicErosion.cut` is private and charges the same square ruler, which by the source's arithmetic reads a north-south slope at 0.50 of the ground's and a diagonal one at 0.79 (at 2048, cells of 5.859375 by 2.9296875 km); on 969495 at 512, 29.4% of land steps run down a column and 37.7% diagonally. Recorded, not established as a cause. In `TODO.md`. **Cost and tier:** `CoastalSpacingAuditTest` is in the audit tier, five cases: the 21-world table 838 s (a 2048 world 79 to 90 s to generate and about a second to measure, one world held at a time), the instrument's scales on six worlds 226 s, the 24 controlled worlds 1,300 s, the floors 12 s and the rulers 8 s. Nothing in the generator moved. | **Renders**, written by the audit to `worldgen/build/maps/` and looked at, not kept: `x1d-969495-2048-fronts.png`, the whole world with every coast's line in red and every mountain front's in orange, straight coasts marked all round both continents and nothing along the map's edges; and `x1d-969495-2048-front1.png` to `front3.png`, the three coasts carrying a trunk spacing whose traced comb is finest, at twice the grid's pixels, trunk basins tinted, trunk outlets yellow and other catchments' outlets cyan. The first is the author's coast: a steep green band a few cells deep along a straight shore, crossed by parallel courses three to five cells apart running straight down it, between five trunks whose tinted basins reach far inland. The second and third, on an Andean coast and a rift coast, show the same: a few trunks with broad basins and, between them, short straight courses down the coastal slope. |
| T5 The per-merge tier in a quarter of its time | 2026-09-23 | chunk/t5-test-tier | **Measured first.** On the sixteen-thread machine, idle, with compilation warm and every test task forced, the tier took **1 h 24 m 42 s**: `:worldgen:jvmTest` 59 m 41 s over 69 classes and 233 tests, `:cartography:jvmTest` 10 m 14 s beside it, and then `:desktop:test` 24 m 50 s — after the generator's suite rather than beside it, because that suite held `:worldgen`'s project lock and the Wasm compilation and package manifests `:desktop` and `:ui` wait on are `:worldgen`'s tasks. A lone worker kept the processor 23% busy on average. A count of `WorldGenerationEngine.generate` calls by their settings, printed by a line in the engine that was never committed, found **590 generations in the generator's suite (2,918 s of its 3,580 s in classes)**, 67 in cartography's and 84 in desktop's; the four standard worlds at 512 were generated 268 times among them, seed 42 alone 64 times in 38 of the generator's classes, and `RiverSelectionTest` made 29 worlds of five settings. **Worlds lent, not made.** `SharedWorlds`, in `worldgen/src/sharedTestSupport` and compiled into the JVM tests of `:worldgen`, `:cartography` and `:desktop`, keys on the whole `WorldGenConfig` — every constructor property of it is a value, an enum or a nested data class, and the only properties in class bodies are computed — and lends only fresh generation on the processor with no progress watched; 41 of the generator's classes, 6 of cartography's and 5 of desktop's borrow through it. Kept out because generation is their subject: `IncrementalReuseTest`, `GenerationStopTest`, the reuse generations of `ChannelInitiationControlTest`, every `Gpu*` class, the interface-driven generations of `ChromeGalleryTest`, `PhoneAtlasTest` and `SeedFieldTest`, the exporter's own in `ExportSmokeTest`, `GenerationProgressTest`, and the `commonTest` classes, which also run on Wasm (`PipelineTest`'s determinism case among them); and the two cases that time a generation, `FlatCourseTest`'s cost and `RiverWidthTest`'s, which were found timing a borrowed world and generate their own again. Nothing but the pool's width is read besides the settings, and the fingerprint lines and every figure 114 classes print came out the same with each worker's pool two threads wide as with fifteen. **The check.** Every lent world is fingerprinted branch by branch by reflection — every array, list and field reachable from the `WorldMap`, a type the walk does not know refused rather than skipped — and checked on every hand-out, a second one to the same test included, after every test of a class that borrowed it, after every such class has finished with its teardown, and before it is dropped; a JUnit 4 class gets the two checks by extending `BorrowsSharedWorlds`, a JUnit 5 one by the `SharedWorldsCheck` extension, and a world asked for outside either is generated for the caller and kept by nobody. An independent review found two holes in the first version, both closed: a shortcut that handed a world straight back to the test already holding it let a test write, borrow again and restore unseen, so every hand-out is checked now (297 checks at 512 in a run, 20 ms each against five to seven seconds a generation); and three classes kept lent worlds in their companion objects (`GroundTextureTest`, `SnowBalanceTest`, `VegetationDensityTest`), which held them past the checks and past the bound, so those borrow on every read instead and a world that leaves the cache is followed by a weak reference and checked while anything holds it. The other companion caches in borrowing classes hold only figures derived from a world (`EarthLikenessTest`, `GlacialBasinShapeTest`, `IceSheetTest`, whose sea-level results `SeaLevelStage.apply` builds in new arrays). Shown on the real cache with two classes written to write and then deleted: the test that wrote into seed 42's temperature failed its own check, naming itself and `climate.temperature`, and the next test was handed a regenerated world; a test that wrote, borrowed again and restored failed at the second borrow; and a write from the `@AfterClass` of the last class of the run, after every test had been checked, failed that class through the runner, naming the test that last held the world and `rivers.flowTarget`. `SharedWorldsGuardTest` keeps the demonstration on a lender of its own. No routed test writes: every run since has passed every check. Retention: 600 MB of arrays (a 512 world is 39 MB), variants dropped before plain worlds — seed 99, which fewer classes ask for, was generated in ten classes of one run under the first rule, which dropped whatever only one class had asked for — and nothing above 1024 by 1024 kept. **Workers.** `:worldgen` four workers of two processors and 3.5 GB each, `:desktop` one of six and 3 GB (its graphics classes drive one card), `:cartography` one of one and 2 GB, `:ui` one of one: 16 threads, and 19.5 GB of worker heaps that can run together (the site's 0.5 GB only after `:desktop:test`), each worker told its share with `-XX:ActiveProcessorCount` and its pool set to match; capping the pool alone and leaving the collector's and compiler's threads to the JVM measured 22 m 42 s against 21 m 34 s. Peaks after a collection: 2.8 GB in the `:worldgen` worker that generated both per-merge 2048 worlds, 2.3 GB in `:desktop`'s, 1.1 GB in `:cartography`'s. `:worldgen:jvmTest` and `:cartography:jvmTest` now `mustRunAfter` their own Wasm tasks, so the rest of the tier starts beside them. Gradle deals classes to workers round-robin in name order, the audit-only ones counted, which reproduces the measured worker sums exactly (741, 710, 1,217 and 1,284 s). `LittoralCoastTest`'s five erosions a grid are made once for the class rather than once a case (123 s to 35 s). **Moved to the audit tier**, none of them asserting anything: `W3RenderDump`, which said it was excluded and was not; `ClimateFedErosionMeasurementTest`; `BorderRealismTest`; `RiftDepthTest`; `RiverEndingsTest`; three report cases by class and method (`SeaLevelHistoryTest`'s table, `LakeWaterBalanceTest`'s budget, `DepositionTest`'s mouth renders); in a new `:cartography:audit`, `W4RenderDump` (six minutes of every merge) and the four render harnesses that only draw on request; in `:desktop:audit`, `GpuOceanTest`'s wall clock at 2048 and 4096 (seven minutes), `StyleGalleryTest`'s engraved renders and `GpuErosionTest`'s drift report. **After, on the same machine alone: 22 m 04 s**, and 22 m 29 s with the review's checks, every task green — 233 tests in the generator's 65 classes, 69 in cartography's 15, 74 in desktop's 19, 21 in the site's, 116 each in the interface's JVM and browser suites — with the four standard worlds generated 32 times rather than 268 and the generator's suite making 349 worlds rather than 590. Of the 637 test cases the tier ran before, 617 are unchanged, 19 moved to the audit tier and pass there, and the one skip moved with them; the audit tier's 63 cases and the Wasm suites' 45 come out as they did, so across all three tiers 725 cases are unchanged, 20 moved with their outcome, 12 are the guard's own and none is lost. **Memory:** the build's processes peaked at 19.2 GB together against 10.3 GB for the one-worker tier, so the 22 minutes cost about 9 GB; each process at its own peak sums to 21.8 GB, and the budget in the root build script, with the workers at their heap ceilings, to 23.4 GB. Fewer workers would give memory back at the cost of time: Gradle's dealing puts 2,311 s of classes on one of three workers against 1,335 s on the worst of four. The audit tasks now run one after another rather than side by side, `:worldgen`, then `:cartography`, then `:desktop`, so the most they hold at once is `:desktop`'s 10 GB and the daemon's; two of them together had taken the nightly's 16 GB runner down twice, and in series the tier takes the sum of the three rather than the longest. The audit tier, holding the moved classes now, took 2 h 0 m against 1 h 40 m. Two runs begun while another build shared the machine lost three workers between them to access violations in garbage-collector threads, all at the same address inside `jvm.dll` and one of them eleven minutes after the other build had ended; the rerun alone passed, and so had the three runs before them. **What is left in the way:** the four workers are uneven because Gradle does not look at cost, one of them carrying `GlaciationTest`'s eighteen distinct 1024 worlds (nine minutes); and desktop's single worker takes 20 minutes on its own, most of it the ocean's parity at 2048, the outlet at three grids and the interface photographed with worlds it generates itself. The end of every run that tests now prints each task's wall time and the slowest classes. |
